AI Media Scout

2026-11-17 · All platforms · Open-source software

30 complete documentation guides and 52 additional screened creative-AI tools/components. 11 earlier screened entries now have guides; these are review completions, not new-launch claims. Workflows include editable agent-authored pixel assets, game art, transcript-based editing, local music sketches, typography/story prompting, OCR and VFX handoff. Every admission has a complete current software-grant review and six explicit quality checks. Required proprietary hosts, restricted/unclear weights and paid services are separated from the code license. Nothing discovered was installed or run. Final query metadata adds textile reconstruction, local filmmaking, agent capture/synchronization and depth-based temporal VFX. A WIF matrix round-trip is not a physical cloth trial, relative depth is not metric geometry, and MiniMax-H3 territory/output terms and Qwen-Image2.1 noncommercial terms are separate from the application’s open code grant. An audio-to-MIDI/ML roadmap without the implemented AI feature stays local. Nine existing-library source follow-ups recover five complete software grants from GitLab without promoting their quality tiers; one current low-poly portrait repository contains only README and LICENSE, so its setup check is explicitly failed. Codeberg bot challenges and unavailable OpenSlides sources keep affected checks unknown. The prior 3000-entry library already has this week’s cited whole-library source reviews; these follow-ups and new records extend that coverage, rather than claiming3000 new reviews today. Final weekly completeness is checked after the library refresh. The full frozen discovery plan covers 108 fields,1177 repository queries and 27 model tasks. It carries every prior term/lane, adds an agent-pixel-art field, event-camera/caustic terms and image classification/feature extraction, deepens74 formerly bounded queries and adds120 date partitions for 40 capped terms. All three repository lanes remain active. Live primary research beyond GitHub supplements every field and all eight configured ecosystems. Metadata-level screening includes the complete retained catalog and historical pilot entries, followed by targeted document/code/full-license reviews beyond the automatic README shortlist; raw unreviewed/excluded records stay local. Two earlier unsealed Codeberg bot-challenge bodies were superseded during recapture; their original attempt metadata is retained with that archival limitation. Later challenge captures and failed source requests are preserved, and no quality or license claim relies on the missing bodies. First-hand support reports add concrete Windows plugin-discovery, ComfyUI setup, EXR color metadata, DXF and cached-model caveats. Owner examples, agent judges, stars and source activity do not establish a recommendation. The Aseprite plugin is represented as optional packaging around its canonical server rather than counted twice. Web/manual shells, source failures and bounded captures are disclosed; retained research abstracts supply context without a claim of complete paper or manual review today. There is no fixed profile/discovery quota, platform preference, app execution or extra scheduler. Verified collection: 1177/1177 repository queries attempted; 1174 successful, 2 partial, 1 failed and 255 bounded/truncated. All 27 model tasks were attempted; 0 failed. These are bounded discovery attempts, not an exhaustive census or quality approval. Empty model-source responses: text2text-generation. An empty response does not establish that these creative workflows have no tools. Separate from successful search queries, 53 watchlist fetches and 4 enrichment attempts failed; the detailed warnings remain in the report. A 404/409 is an availability gap, not proof of abandonment or license revocation.

Unknown hardware requirements stay unknown. Code, model weights, hosted services, and required proprietary hosts can have different terms.

Pixelbolt

Documented creative workflow; quality confidence assessed separately · MIT · first-profile

First complete guide in this library, including completion of any earlier screened entry; not a claim of a launch today.

Docs checked 2026-11-17 · 116 GitHub stars · Release v0.17.0 (2026-11-14) · Documentation review; installation not tested

Quality assessment: Quality unverified

Software licensing and a concrete creative AI use are supported. The remaining checks do not establish a recommendation.

Scope: Making small editable sprites, tiles, interface icons and short pixel effects, with exact palette revisions and transparent exports for games or video composition.

Evidence checked 2026-11-17; documentation review does not imply hands-on testing.

  • Open-source software license · verified: The complete root software license was read and archived. The grant covers this project code; unresolved model, service and asset terms are disclosed separately. [2]
  • Creative results · documented: The owner supplies source recipes, animated galleries and a controlled comparison on older version 0.11.0. That study did not meet its preregistered threshold for claiming an advantage over direct agent coding; it is not independent proof of current 0.17 output quality. Inspect every frame and loop at native resolution. [1] [3] [4]
  • Reproducible setup · documented: Use the official portable Studio archive for Windows x64, Linux x64/ARM64 or macOS Intel/Apple Silicon, or clone the source with Node 20+. The portable build bundles Node 24 LTS. No npm install or image-model download is needed for the renderer. Connect your chosen AI host separately; the optional skills CLI has a distinct Node 22.20+ requirement and telemetry behavior. Independent reproduction has not been established in this review. [1] [3] [4]
  • Maintenance and support · documented: Current release/commit and bounded issue/PR evidence were inspected for workflow support. The query returned 10 records, including owner/bot items; it is not an exhaustive issue survey and establishes no general support/reliability clearance. [5]
  • Independent use · unknown: No successful independent use of the complete selected workflow was established in the bounded support/primary-document review. Owner automation, bots, adjacent-workflow reports and popularity are not substituted. [5]
  • Models, services and costs · documented: The complete MIT software grant was reviewed; the README also covers original included artwork under MIT. There are no renderer model weights. The chosen AI host/model and any imported media retain separate terms. The renderer is local and needs no hosted inference service or key. A chosen AI authoring host can require its own account, model compute or paid access; optional paid effect packs are separate from the MIT toolkit. [1] [3] [4]

Documentation review only; AI Media Scout did not install or run this software. Animation frames and imports are limited to 256x256; projects have at most 256 frames and 4,194,304 source pixels. A single still can reach 4096 pixels per side within the total; scenes and exports have additional limits. Advisory validation does not judge aesthetics. MCP calls have time/memory/output limits; large exports can require the CLI. URL sharing and embedded recipes carry source text. No universal RAM, VRAM or disk minimum is documented.

How it uses AI

An AI agent authors and patches editable palette-based JSON through CLI or MCP tools; Pixelbolt deterministically rasterizes sprites, scenes and animations and returns pixel grids/contact sheets for the next agent revision. Its fluid and rigid-body effects are conventional simulations, not image-model inference. [1] [3] [4]

Introduction

An AI agent authors and patches editable palette-based JSON through CLI or MCP tools; Pixelbolt deterministically rasterizes sprites, scenes and animations and returns pixel grids/contact sheets for the next agent revision. Its fluid and rigid-body effects are conventional simulations, not image-model inference. [1] [3] [4]

What it is good for

Making small editable sprites, tiles, interface icons and short pixel effects, with exact palette revisions and transparent exports for games or video composition. [1] [3] [4]

Demo & examples

The owner supplies source recipes, animated galleries and a controlled comparison on older version 0.11.0. That study did not meet its preregistered threshold for claiming an advantage over direct agent coding; it is not independent proof of current 0.17 output quality. Inspect every frame and loop at native resolution. [1] [3] [4]

Install

Use the official portable Studio archive for Windows x64, Linux x64/ARM64 or macOS Intel/Apple Silicon, or clone the source with Node 20+. The portable build bundles Node 24 LTS. No npm install or image-model download is needed for the renderer. Connect your chosen AI host separately; the optional skills CLI has a distinct Node 22.20+ requirement and telemetry behavior. [1] [3] [4]

  1. Obtain the official source or release linked below.
  2. Use the official portable Studio archive for Windows x64, Linux x64/ARM64 or macOS Intel/Apple Silicon, or clone the source with Node 20+. The portable build bundles Node 24 LTS. No npm install or image-model download is needed for the renderer. Connect your chosen AI host separately; the optional skills CLI has a distinct Node 22.20+ requirement and telemetry behavior.
git clone https://github.com/skulitom/Pixelbolt.git
cd Pixelbolt
node bin/pixelbolt.js preview --open

First project

Start with a short, representative project and inspect the result before a larger batch. [1] [3] [4]

  1. Open the local studio, choose a small supplied recipe and save your own JSON copy.
  2. Have your agent alter a palette, one region or a short motion; validate the recipe and inspect its native-size frames, silhouette and loop.
  3. Render into a new output folder, keeping the JSON recipe and atlas with the PNG/APNG assets. GIF export can alter alpha and timing.
  4. For a video editor, export a constant-rate numbered PNG sequence and set that rate on import. Pixelbolt supplies FFmpeg commands but does not itself encode an MP4.

Hardware & software

Requirements below are documented for this workflow. Unspecified limits are not inferred. [1] [3] [4]

Hardware
A CPU renderer is documented without a GPU/image-model requirement. Minimum RAM and total storage are not documented. The portable runtime download size is not a system-memory minimum.
Software
Node 20+ for source/CLI/MCP, or a portable Studio build with its runtime. A separate compatible AI agent is the creative author. FFmpeg is optional for turning exported frames into video.
Platforms
Windows, macOS and Linux are explicitly documented, with five portable OS/architecture targets. CI content checks do not prove a human double-click, download-warning or every desktop/browser route works.

License, model weights & costs

The full software license was reviewed. Model weights, assets and services remain separate. [2] [1] [3] [4]

  • Weights: The complete MIT software grant was reviewed; the README also covers original included artwork under MIT. There are no renderer model weights. The chosen AI host/model and any imported media retain separate terms.
  • Commercial: Software use follows MIT. The complete MIT software grant was reviewed; the README also covers original included artwork under MIT. There are no renderer model weights. The chosen AI host/model and any imported media retain separate terms.
  • Cost: The renderer is local and needs no hosted inference service or key. A chosen AI authoring host can require its own account, model compute or paid access; optional paid effect packs are separate from the MIT toolkit.

Why it merits attention

Included for the documented creative workflow. A complete guide and popularity do not establish output reliability. [1] [3] [4]

Limitations

Animation frames and imports are limited to 256x256; projects have at most 256 frames and 4,194,304 source pixels. A single still can reach 4096 pixels per side within the total; scenes and exports have additional limits. Advisory validation does not judge aesthetics. MCP calls have time/memory/output limits; large exports can require the CLI. URL sharing and embedded recipes carry source text. No universal RAM, VRAM or disk minimum is documented. [1] [3] [4]

pxl — editable pixel art and critique fork

Documented creative workflow; quality confidence assessed separately · MIT · recently-created

The repository was created on 2026-10-21. This is its first full guide in this library, not independent validation of a new release.

Docs checked 2026-11-17 · 72 GitHub stars · Documentation review; installation not tested

Quality assessment: Quality unverified

Software licensing and a concrete creative AI use are supported. The remaining checks do not establish a recommendation.

Scope: Iterating small game sprites, palette variants, short animations and isometric scenes while keeping the art as editable source.

Evidence checked 2026-11-17; documentation review does not imply hands-on testing.

  • Open-source software license · verified: The complete root software license was read and archived. The grant covers this project code; unresolved model, service and asset terms are disclosed separately. [2]

There are no runtime dependencies or source build step; npm development/type-check dependencies are separate. Documentation review only; AI Media Scout did not install or run this software. This is a substantial fork of crsolver/claude-pxl, not an unrelated invention; the upstream is credited rather than counted as a second overlapping admission. Lint detects specified pixel patterns, not taste. Arbitrary art source is code and belongs in a trusted local project. Node-version and native-viewer compatibility, hardware minima and independent reproduction remain unverified.

How it uses AI

A coding agent writes a compact JavaScript/TypeScript pixel-art scene, renders it through a deterministic DSL, inspects exact grids and lint overlays, and patches the source. The fork adds hue-shift ramps, selective outlines and annotated human feedback; its rendering and lint are not learned image generation. [1] [3]

Introduction

A coding agent writes a compact JavaScript/TypeScript pixel-art scene, renders it through a deterministic DSL, inspects exact grids and lint overlays, and patches the source. The fork adds hue-shift ramps, selective outlines and annotated human feedback; its rendering and lint are not learned image generation. [1] [3]

What it is good for

Iterating small game sprites, palette variants, short animations and isometric scenes while keeping the art as editable source. [1] [3]

Demo & examples

The owner publishes each gallery image with its generating file, plus mushroom lint and annotated portrait examples. Those show inspectable owner workflows, not independently verified artistic quality or universal game-engine compatibility. [1] [3]

Install

Clone the substantive uilianks fork and use Node 22.18+ to run its CLI directly. There are no runtime dependencies or source build step; npm development/type-check dependencies are separate. The README quick-start cd name still refers to the upstream name, so use the actual cloned pxl directory. [1] [3]

git clone https://github.com/uilianks/pxl.git

First project

Start with a short, representative project and inspect the result before a larger batch. [1] [3]

  1. Open the live viewer and copy a small example to a new .px.js file.
  2. Ask an AI agent to edit the explicit palette, sprite grid and frame patches, then inspect at native size.
  3. Run the documented lint/ASCII inspection commands and review coordinates rather than accepting an aesthetic pass automatically.
  4. Build PNGs, atlas JSON and GIFs; use the documented Godot option when you need SpriteFrames resources, then inspect timing in the destination engine.

Minimum system RAM, VRAM and total disk space are not documented in the reviewed instructions.

Node 22.18+; an external coding agent for AI authoring. A browser is used by the optional local viewer. Development-only TypeScript tooling is separate.
Platforms
The README documents a Node/local-browser workflow but no independently tested OS/architecture matrix. Browser access alone does not certify Mac, Windows or Linux support.

  • Commercial: Software use follows MIT. Complete MIT original/fork software grant and notices reviewed, preserving crsolver upstream attribution. No embedded neural weights; AI-host and imported-image/font/palette rights remain separate.
  • Cost: Local compute plus any chosen external model or service; provider access and charges are separate.

How it uses AI

An AI agent composes declarative JSON for a deterministic Python/Pillow renderer: palette grids, isometric structures, tiles and animation frames become inspectable PNG/GIF/game assets. Integer geometry and palette locking are conventional rendering controls, not an image model. [1] [3]

Introduction

An AI agent composes declarative JSON for a deterministic Python/Pillow renderer: palette grids, isometric structures, tiles and animation frames become inspectable PNG/GIF/game assets. Integer geometry and palette locking are conventional rendering controls, not an image model. [1] [3]

What it is good for

Drafting small isometric architecture, autotiles and character animation with source specs embedded in exported PNGs for later agent edits. [1] [3]

First project

Start with a short, representative project and inspect the result before a larger batch. [1] [3]

  1. Start from a small supplied JSON spec and let the agent change one shape or palette region.
  2. Render the spec to a new PNG path, then inspect its bounding box, colors and ASCII preview.
  3. For animation, export GIF or a sheet with metadata; the documented Godot option writes a SpriteFrames resource.
  4. Extract the embedded source from your generated PNG to revise it, keeping the original and inspecting the final game import manually.

Local container/model compute plus charges and access requirements for the chosen optional image/video provider. Conventional extraction can work without a cloud key. [1] [3]

Vertex is selected automatically when its project variable is configured; model choices and backend availability differ. Default development configuration enables Flask debugging, so the quick start is not a vetted public deployment. The upload cap is not a memory requirement. Total RAM, GPU and storage minima remain undocumented.

How it uses AI

Gemini image generation and Veo video generation are connected to a sprite asset library; local rembg foreground removal and FFmpeg frame extraction support cleanup and export. Color-key, crop and resize tools are conventional controls. [1] [3]

Introduction

Gemini image generation and Veo video generation are connected to a sprite asset library; local rembg foreground removal and FFmpeg frame extraction support cleanup and export. Color-key, crop and resize tools are conventional controls. [1] [3]

What it is good for

Iterating a sprite from a prompt/reference, optionally animating it through a chosen cloud video model, and extracting cleaned frames for a game asset. [1] [3]

Demo & examples

The README documents the generation/refinement/library and video-to-frames workflows. Cloud availability, character consistency, timing and actual engine integration are not independently validated. [1] [3]

Install

Build the documented Docker Compose application and open the local web UI on port 5000. The first build downloads u2net, about 176 MB. Configure either Google AI Studio or Vertex AI for generation; local extraction/editing does not require those keys. Vertex requires separate project/service-account configuration. [1] [3]

  1. Obtain the official source or release linked below.
  2. Build the documented Docker Compose application and open the local web UI on port 5000. The first build downloads u2net, about 176 MB. Configure either Google AI Studio or Vertex AI for generation; local extraction/editing does not require those keys. Vertex requires separate project/service-account configuration.
docker-compose up --build

First project

Start with a short, representative project and inspect the result before a larger batch. [1] [3]

  1. Start with owned art or a short permitted input clip and create an asset in the library.
  2. Configure the chosen provider if you want neural generation; save a useful draft before asking for variations or animation.
  3. Choose a crop/time range, extract frames and preview motion at the intended rate.
  4. Apply color or rembg transparency, inspect fine details and save/export PNGs or a ZIP. Check the destination engine separately.

A Docker/local-browser route is documented; an independently tested OS/architecture matrix is not. A browser UI does not itself establish Mac compatibility.

How it uses AI

The toolkit conventionally chroma-keys and slices AI-generated pixel drafts into PNG/atlas/manifest artifacts. Neural image creation is an upstream, separately supplied step; this code makes the handoff inspectable rather than generating images itself. [1] [3] [4]

Introduction

The toolkit conventionally chroma-keys and slices AI-generated pixel drafts into PNG/atlas/manifest artifacts. Neural image creation is an upstream, separately supplied step; this code makes the handoff inspectable rather than generating images itself. [1] [3] [4]

What it is good for

Preparing generated sprite drafts for a separately reviewed game import while preserving raw inputs and metadata. [1] [3] [4]

Demo & examples

The owner supplies AgentForge samples and related runtime proof artifacts. NOTICE explicitly separates those from visual/fun acceptance and final shipped art; no independent production-quality evidence is established. [1] [3] [4]

Install

Clone the source and use an isolated Python 3.10+ environment, then install the editable package with Pillow 10+. Begin with a supplied or owned draft and new output paths. [1] [3] [4]

  1. Obtain the official source or release linked below.
  2. Clone the source and use an isolated Python 3.10+ environment, then install the editable package with Pillow 10+. Begin with a supplied or owned draft and new output paths.
python3 -m venv .venv
source .venv/bin/activate
python -m pip install -e .
codex-asset-forge key examples/agentforge-starter/raw/tileset_raw.png /tmp/tileset_keyed.png
codex-asset-forge slice-grid /tmp/tileset_keyed.png /tmp/tiles --columns 2 --rows 2 --prefix tile
codex-asset-forge manifest-grid /tmp/tileset_keyed.png /tmp/tileset.json --columns 2 --rows 2 --layer base

First project

Start with a short, representative project and inspect the result before a larger batch. [1] [3] [4]

  1. Retain the unmodified generated draft and confirm that its background is appropriate for chroma keying.
  2. Key a copy and inspect alpha edges and any subject colors close to the background.
  3. Slice using the actual grid and write a manifest with the intended layer; verify rectangles and anchors.
  4. Import into the destination runtime and inspect actual motion, placement and collision behavior; keep human acceptance separate from file/renderer proof.

How it uses AI

ai_emb.py learns a per-image KMeans color palette, finds contours and emits rotated hatch geometry as SVG. This is classical image clustering plus conventional geometry, not neural stitch generation. [1] [3] [4] [5]

Introduction

ai_emb.py learns a per-image KMeans color palette, finds contours and emits rotated hatch geometry as SVG. This is classical image clustering plus conventional geometry, not neural stitch generation. [1] [3] [4] [5]

What it is good for

Studying color reduction and vector hatch patterns before a separately reviewed embroidery workflow. [1] [3] [4] [5]

Demo & examples

The source exposes the actual KMeans/SVG path. Its companion app.py is a conventional grayscale-contour DST path; no machine stitching, calibrated dimensions or independent production proof is established. [1] [3] [4] [5]

Install

Obtain the source, isolate Python dependencies from requirements.txt and manually configure ai_emb.py input/parameters before running it. The README is only a heading and the unrelated batch launcher points to a missing file, so this is a source-editing route rather than a turnkey GUI. [1] [3] [4] [5]

  1. Obtain the official source or release linked below.
  2. Obtain the source, isolate Python dependencies from requirements.txt and manually configure ai_emb.py input/parameters before running it. The README is only a heading and the unrelated batch launcher points to a missing file, so this is a source-editing route rather than a turnkey GUI.

First project

Start with a short, representative project and inspect the result before a larger batch. [1] [3] [4] [5]

  3. Inspect SVG color regions, tiny contours and hatch density at the intended dimensions.
  4. Treat any eventual stitch conversion, stabilization and physical sew-out as a separate unverified process.

How it uses AI

Introduction

An AI coding agent operates a structured local editing CLI; optional faster-whisper transcribes speech, while plans, cuts, captions and FFmpeg renders are deterministic editing operations. [1] [3] [4]

What it is good for

  1. Obtain the official source or release linked below.

python -m pip install --upgrade "moviestar[transcribe]"
moviestar doctor
moviestar probe /path/to/video.mp4
moviestar load /path/to/video.mp4 --as source

First project

Start with a short, representative project and inspect the result before a larger batch. [1] [3] [4]

  3. Review export --dry-run before rendering into a fresh destination.
  4. Inspect the exported video and audio; a screenshot/probe alone does not verify motion or sound.

OCRacle

Documented creative workflow; quality confidence assessed separately · MIT · recently-created

The repository was created on 2026-10-19. This is its first full guide in this library, not independent validation of a new release.

Docs checked 2026-11-17 · 0 GitHub stars · Release 1.2.1 (2026-11-13) · Documentation review; installation not tested

Quality assessment: Experimental

Software licensing and a concrete creative AI use are supported. The remaining checks do not establish a recommendation.

Scope: Researching card-text preservation and OCR behavior on stylized, damaged or italic Latin-script print.

Evidence checked 2026-11-17; documentation review does not imply hands-on testing.

  • Why Experimental: The current usage guide explicitly calls the OCR system experimental and records incomplete layout, symbols and non-Latin/historic-font coverage. [1] [3] [4] [5]
  • Open-source software license · verified: The complete root software license was read and archived. The grant covers this project code; unresolved model, service and asset terms are disclosed separately. [2]
  • Creative results · documented: The owner publishes fixed crops, literal references, actual recorded predictions and failures. The selected medium baseline reports 314/448 exact lines and 2.78% CER on one development comparison, with clean regressions; this is not universal language/font accuracy or independent user validation. [1] [3] [4] [5]
  • Reproducible setup · documented: Clone the source, use Rust 1.94+ and Python 3 for the initial hash-pinned model downloader, then run the recognize CLI. Recognition runs locally after required model downloads. Independent reproduction has not been established in this review. [1] [3] [4] [5]
  • Maintenance and support · documented: Current release/commit and bounded issue/PR evidence were inspected for workflow support. The query returned 10 records, including owner/bot items; it is not an exhaustive issue survey and establishes no general support/reliability clearance. [6]
  • Independent use · unknown: No successful independent use of the complete selected workflow was established in the bounded support/primary-document review. Owner automation, bots, adjacent-workflow reports and popularity are not substituted. [6]
  • Models, services and costs · documented: Complete MIT code grant reviewed. Paddle models retain Apache 2 notices; adapted/original Ocrs weights are separately CC BY-SA4.0. Wizards card text/art and scans are not licensed by the code grant. Local compute plus any chosen external model or service; provider access and charges are separate. [1] [3] [4] [5]

Documentation review only; AI Media Scout did not install or run this software. The current project explicitly remains experimental. Six Latin-script languages have stronger medium-baseline evidence; Latin la and Japanese/Chinese remain pending/best effort. Mana symbols, vertical/curved text, whole-card layout and severe blur are incomplete; RAM/VRAM/storage minima are undocumented.

How it uses AI

Local Rust CTC neural OCR reads printed Magic card pixels, including historic typography and italics. Language profiles choose scoped recognizers; it does not substitute a card database lookup for observed text. [1] [3] [4] [5]

Introduction

Local Rust CTC neural OCR reads printed Magic card pixels, including historic typography and italics. Language profiles choose scoped recognizers; it does not substitute a card database lookup for observed text. [1] [3] [4] [5]

What it is good for

Researching card-text preservation and OCR behavior on stylized, damaged or italic Latin-script print. [1] [3] [4] [5]

Demo & examples

The owner publishes fixed crops, literal references, actual recorded predictions and failures. The selected medium baseline reports 314/448 exact lines and 2.78% CER on one development comparison, with clean regressions; this is not universal language/font accuracy or independent user validation. [1] [3] [4] [5]

Install

Clone the source, use Rust 1.94+ and Python 3 for the initial hash-pinned model downloader, then run the recognize CLI. Recognition runs locally after required model downloads. [1] [3] [4] [5]

  1. Obtain the official source or release linked below.
  2. Clone the source, use Rust 1.94+ and Python 3 for the initial hash-pinned model downloader, then run the recognize CLI. Recognition runs locally after required model downloads.
python3 training/download_models.py
cargo run --release -- recognize card.jpg

First project

Start with a short, representative project and inspect the result before a larger batch. [1] [3] [4] [5]

  1. Begin with an owned, upright crop and the correct printed-language profile.
  2. Use --line for an already cropped line; retain transforms if integrating a camera pipeline.
  3. Compare JSON transcription, coordinates and actual model identities against the printed reference.
  4. Keep accents, punctuation and severe-blur failures visible; confidence is an uncalibrated model score.

Minimum system RAM, VRAM and total disk space are not documented in the reviewed instructions.

Platforms
No complete tested desktop OS/architecture matrix is documented in the reviewed guide.

  • Commercial: Software use follows MIT. Complete MIT code grant reviewed. Paddle models retain Apache 2 notices; adapted/original Ocrs weights are separately CC BY-SA4.0. Wizards card text/art and scans are not licensed by the code grant.

First project

  4. Save a .forge.json project and inspect PNG/GIF or sheet/JSON exports in the intended engine.

How it uses AI

Whisper.net runs local neural speech transcription; optional Ollama or Hugging Face models polish text, while conventional recording/file conversion supplies audio. [1]

Introduction

Whisper.net runs local neural speech transcription; optional Ollama or Hugging Face models polish text, while conventional recording/file conversion supplies audio. [1]

What it is good for

Drafting text from owned interviews, podcasts and voice recordings with an explicit formatting-provider choice. [1]

Demo & examples

The owner documents recording, file transcription, local formatting and browser examples. Heuristic speaker grouping and formatting prose are not independent transcription/diarization quality evidence. [1]

Install

For source use, install the documented .NET10 SDK and build/run the project. Select a Windows recording device or supply an existing audio file; allow the chosen GGML model download. Docker is a separate file/web route, not a Windows audio-capture replacement. [1]

  1. Obtain the official source or release linked below.
  2. For source use, install the documented .NET10 SDK and build/run the project. Select a Windows recording device or supply an existing audio file; allow the chosen GGML model download. Docker is a separate file/web route, not a Windows audio-capture replacement.

First project

Start with a short, representative project and inspect the result before a larger batch. [1]

  1. List devices before recording on Windows, or select a short owned audio file.
  2. Choose a documented Whisper model and explicit formatting provider; use the local deterministic formatter when remote polishing is unwanted.
  3. Transcribe a representative clip and compare names, numbers, omissions and speaker changes against the audio.
  4. Save the Markdown/transcript and keep original audio plus model/settings for review.

Platforms
Windows 10/11 WASAPI recording is explicit. Docker CPU/CUDA file-transcription/web workflows are declared across other platforms; that does not establish Mac/Linux recording support.

How it uses AI

A Go MCP server lets an AI assistant operate Aseprite drawing, layers, indexed palettes, animation and export. Palette quantization, shading and dithering are conventional processing; the external agent supplies AI authorship. [1] [3] [4] [5] [6]

Introduction

A Go MCP server lets an AI assistant operate Aseprite drawing, layers, indexed palettes, animation and export. Palette quantization, shading and dithering are conventional processing; the external agent supplies AI authorship. [1] [3] [4] [5] [6]

What it is good for

Building editable sprites and short animation sheets through a configurable Aseprite tool interface. [1] [3] [4] [5] [6]

Demo & examples

The owner supplies a 64x64 indexed scene and a comprehensive workflow example. Advertised sub 100ms operations are owner measurements, not a general performance or artistic-quality guarantee. [1] [3] [4] [5] [6]

Install

Obtain the source, use the current go.mod baseline 1.24.1 and build the server; the README requirements table still says Go 1.23+. Configure an absolute Aseprite path in ~/.config/pixel-mcp/config.json, then register the built binary as a stdio MCP server. Aseprite 1.3.0+ is declared, with 1.3.10+ recommended by the owner.   3. Inspect actual pixels and frame timing while adding a short motion.
  4. Export PNG/GIF or a sheet and verify the native .aseprite file plus destination-engine import.

The owner declares Windows/macOS/Linux. Independent platform/build and host-integration coverage remains incomplete.

pixscii

Documented creative workflow; quality confidence assessed separately · MIT · first-profile

First complete guide in this library, including completion of any earlier screened entry; not a claim of a launch today.

Software licensing and a concrete creative AI use are supported. The remaining checks do not establish a recommendation.

Scope: Making small palette-based scenes and animation sheets without an Aseprite host or an embedded image-generation model.

Evidence checked 2026-11-17; documentation review does not imply hands-on testing.

  • Open-source software license · verified: The complete root software license was read and archived. The grant covers this project code; unresolved model, service and asset terms are disclosed separately. [2]
  • Creative results · documented: The owner provides a three-character animation request and procedural scene examples. Their low-latency/quality claims are not independently reproduced; procedural character seeds and a frame-pure core do not guarantee appealing animation. [1] [3]
  • Reproducible setup · documented: Use a Node/npx MCP client route. The current package requires Node>=18.17.0 and depends on the MCP SDK, Sharp and Zod; its browser core is a separate pure-TypeScript export without Node/Sharp/MCP imports. Independent reproduction has not been established in this review. [1] [3]
  • Maintenance and support · documented: Current release/commit and bounded issue/PR evidence were inspected for workflow support. The query returned 6 records, including owner/bot items; it is not an exhaustive issue survey and establishes no general support/reliability clearance. [4]

  • Models, services and costs · documented: Complete MIT grant reviewed. AI-host/model terms, imported images and bundled asset provenance remain separate review questions. Local compute plus any chosen external model or service; provider access and charges are separate. [1] [3]

Documentation review only; AI Media Scout did not install or run this software. Procedural pixel rendering is distinct from learned image generation. Native image I/O, MCP/client compatibility, maximum practical scene sizes, hardware minima and independent results remain unverified. The owner’s zero-latency wording is not a physical performance guarantee.

How it uses AI

An LLM turns a scene description into structured MCP drawing/character/motion calls. A deterministic TypeScript engine composes procedural sprites, palettes and frame sequences; no image model runs inside that engine. [1] [3]

Introduction

An LLM turns a scene description into structured MCP drawing/character/motion calls. A deterministic TypeScript engine composes procedural sprites, palettes and frame sequences; no image model runs inside that engine. [1] [3]

What it is good for

Making small palette-based scenes and animation sheets without an Aseprite host or an embedded image-generation model. [1] [3]

Demo & examples

The owner provides a three-character animation request and procedural scene examples. Their low-latency/quality claims are not independently reproduced; procedural character seeds and a frame-pure core do not guarantee appealing animation. [1] [3]

Install

Use a Node/npx MCP client route. The current package requires Node>=18.17.0 and depends on the MCP SDK, Sharp and Zod; its browser core is a separate pure-TypeScript export without Node/Sharp/MCP imports. [1] [3]

  1. Obtain the official source or release linked below.
  2. Use a Node/npx MCP client route. The current package requires Node>=18.17.0 and depends on the MCP SDK, Sharp and Zod; its browser core is a separate pure-TypeScript export without Node/Sharp/MCP imports.
npx pixscii

First project

Start with a short, representative project and inspect the result before a larger batch. [1] [3]

  1. Register npx -y pixscii in a compatible MCP host and request a small canvas/explicit palette.
  2. Have the agent create one character or a short structured scene rather than assuming a prompt-to-image diffusion call.
  3. Inspect the returned grid/PNG and compare frames for silhouette, palette and motion continuity.
  4. Export a PNG or spritesheet through the documented tools and inspect the actual game import; retain scene parameters.

Tero Subtitler — local transcription and subtitle revision

Documented creative workflow; quality confidence assessed separately · MPL-2.0 · first-profile

First complete guide in this library, including completion of any earlier screened entry; not a claim of a launch today.

Docs checked 2026-11-17 · 471 GitHub stars · Release nightly (2026-11-16) · Documentation review; installation not tested

Quality assessment: Quality unverified

Software licensing and a concrete creative AI use are supported. The remaining checks do not establish a recommendation.

Scope: Drafting accessible subtitles from local speech, then manually correcting words, language changes, timing and line breaks in a subtitle editor.

Evidence checked 2026-11-17; documentation review does not imply hands-on testing.

  • Open-source software license · verified: The complete root software license was read and archived. The grant covers this project code; unresolved model, service and asset terms are disclosed separately. [2]
  • Creative results · documented: An independent report posted in 2025 describes regular faster-whisper/batch subtitling on multilingual episodes, with missed quiet/noisy speech even with medium/large-v3. Rechecking that source today establishes historical use, not an independent reproduction of the current UW backend or a measured accuracy pass. The source UI exposes smart segmentation, mixed-language detection and speaker labels; these controls are not a guarantee of correct dialogue or identity. [1] [3] [4] [5] [6] [7] [8]
  • Reproducible setup · documented: Obtain the official Tero package for the intended platform and inspect its release designation: the captured latest item is nightly 1.0.2.4, not a verified stable release. How it uses AI

Tero combines editable subtitle timing/layout with local Whisper speech transcription and optional Tesseract OCR. This guide scopes the faster-whisper WAV-to-SRT route and the source-documented Audio to text controls; cloud translation/TTS are separate choices. [1] [3] [4] [5] [6] [7] [8]

Introduction

Tero combines editable subtitle timing/layout with local Whisper speech transcription and optional Tesseract OCR. This guide scopes the faster-whisper WAV-to-SRT route and the source-documented Audio to text controls; cloud translation/TTS are separate choices. [1] [3] [4] [5] [6] [7] [8]

What it is good for

Drafting accessible subtitles from local speech, then manually correcting words, language changes, timing and line breaks in a subtitle editor. [1] [3] [4] [5] [6] [7] [8]

Demo & examples

An independent report posted in 2025 describes regular faster-whisper/batch subtitling on multilingual episodes, with missed quiet/noisy speech even with medium/large-v3. Rechecking that source today establishes historical use, not an independent reproduction of the current UW backend or a measured accuracy pass. The source UI exposes smart segmentation, mixed-language detection and speaker labels; these controls are not a guarantee of correct dialogue or identity. [1] [3] [4] [5] [6] [7] [8]

First project

Start with a short, representative project and inspect the result before a larger batch. [1] [3] [4] [5] [6] [7] [8]

  1. Use a short local WAV with representative speech and language changes. The standalone binary expects PCM WAV and resamples to 16 kHz mono; arbitrary container input requires the source/PyAV route, rather than being assumed for the prebuilt binary.
  2. Choose the backend/model explicitly. Missing faster-whisper models download from Hugging Face. Open the SRT in Tero with the corresponding local media and revise text, timing, segmentation and export format.
  3. For integrated transcription, the inspected Audio to text form has Engine, Model, Language and Track controls, a model-download button, Generate, and load-after/add-while-transcribing choices. Its implementation rejects HTTP media for this operation and requires FFmpeg and the selected backend; use a local file.
  4. For code-switched speech, the documented faster-whisper mixed-language option detects each 30-second window; do not force one language for the whole clip. Translate to English changes the task and is distinct from transcription in the source language.
  5. Check quiet lines, noise, repetitions and speaker attribution against the original audio. Preserve a revised subtitle copy and verify timing/line breaks in the target player; an automatic QC profile does not certify accessibility or translation quality.

Hardware & software

Requirements below are documented for this workflow. Unspecified limits are not inferred. [1] [3] [4] [5] [6] [7] [8]

Hardware
System RAM, VRAM and total application/model storage minima are not documented for this complete workflow. The optional Windows CUDA library download is about 1.3 GB and the optional OpenVINO add-on about 200 MB; these are download sizes, not RAM minima.
Software
The prebuilt backend avoids a source Python installation. Optional NVIDIA faster-whisper acceleration needs CUDA 12/cuDNN 9; auto can fall back to CPU and logs the reason. Its macOS route runs on CPU. The distinct whisper.cpp wrapper supports Metal on Apple Silicon and Vulkan on Windows/Linux x64; these are not faster-whisper CUDA on Mac. Tero source compilation uses Lazarus plus the separately listed internal/external packages.
Platforms
The app owner declares Windows/macOS/Linux; app minimum OS versions and complete device testing are not established here. Faster-whisper backend packages separately declare Windows x64, macOS 11+ Intel/Apple Silicon and Linux x64 glibc 2.35+. Its legacy Intel macOS 10.11–10.15 build omits VAD and speaker identification; backend support does not establish that the entire current GUI runs on those older systems.

Time Cube — AI-depth temporal video sculpture

The repository was created on 2026-11-17. This is its first full guide in this library, not independent validation of a new release.

Quality assessment: Quality unverified

Software licensing and a concrete creative AI use are supported. The remaining checks do not establish a recommendation.

Scope: Making temporal video sculptures, depth trails and transparent VFX plates from owned clips; this is an artistic reconstruction route rather than calibrated surveying.

Evidence checked 2026-11-17; documentation review does not imply hands-on testing.

  • Creative results · documented: The owner links a tutorial and examples for depth points, object following and ordinary frame stacks. Owner synthetic tests report camera-path and mask errors on particular rendered scenes; those are not independent results on arbitrary footage. The current depth.py stretches relative predictions between per-frame quantiles and smooths them, so its scene units are not measured metres. [1] [3] [4]
  • Reproducible setup · documented: Use the owner-documented Windows 10/11 route with Python 3.11+, Chrome or Edge/WebGL2, and ffmpeg/ffprobe on PATH. The basic cube needs no Python third-party packages. For neural depth/Track, first install a CUDA-enabled PyTorch matching the NVIDIA device using the linked upstream selector, then the requirements file; launch from that environment. The separate caption environment and Qwen file are optional and are not needed for a first depth-trail plate. No setup command was executed here. Independent reproduction has not been established in this review. [1] [3] [4]

Demo footage has separate Pexels/CC0 credits; MIT code does not license arbitrary input media. How it uses AI

Local Depth Anything depth estimation and CLIPSeg/SAM object selection turn video frames into sculptural stacks and point trails. The procedural cube is separate from optional neural depth, tracking and Qwen3-VL captions. [1] [3] [4]

Introduction

Local Depth Anything depth estimation and CLIPSeg/SAM object selection turn video frames into sculptural stacks and point trails. The procedural cube is separate from optional neural depth, tracking and Qwen3-VL captions. [1] [3] [4]

What it is good for

Making temporal video sculptures, depth trails and transparent VFX plates from owned clips; this is an artistic reconstruction route rather than calibrated surveying. [1] [3] [4]

Demo & examples

The owner links a tutorial and examples for depth points, object following and ordinary frame stacks. Owner synthetic tests report camera-path and mask errors on particular rendered scenes; those are not independent results on arbitrary footage. The current depth.py stretches relative predictions between per-frame quantiles and smooths them, so its scene units are not measured metres. [1] [3] [4]

Install

Use the owner-documented Windows 10/11 route with Python 3.11+, Chrome or Edge/WebGL2, and ffmpeg/ffprobe on PATH. The basic cube needs no Python third-party packages. For neural depth/Track, first install a CUDA-enabled PyTorch matching the NVIDIA device using the linked upstream selector, then the requirements file; launch from that environment. The separate caption environment and Qwen file are optional and are not needed for a first depth-trail plate. No setup command was executed here. [1] [3] [4]

First project

Start with a short, representative project and inspect the result before a larger batch. [1] [3] [4]

  1. Open a short local video. Start with the basic cube to check decoding and choose its In/Out range; brightness-derived relief is a conventional fallback, not AI depth.
  2. Choose Build AI depth, wait for its separate model download/build and inspect depth edges. The current code uses LiheYoung/depth-anything-large-hf, rather than assuming this is Depth Anything V2.
  3. Try Depth points or Depth sheets. For subject isolation, pause on a clear object, Find it with text or click a point, then Follow it; the tracker may release the object when it leaves or becomes uncertain.
  4. Use Camera path only as an artistic estimate: the default 65-degree field of view is assumed, and depth is relative. Orbit/Walk/Turn are synthetic paths and are explicitly different from the measured-from-video option.
  5. Choose frame-by-frame export for a plate. Opaque export is H.264 MP4; the documented transparent route is ProRes 4444 MOV or VP9-alpha WebM. Live Record stays opaque. Check alpha, timing, contours and sound in the actual receiving editor.

Hardware & software

Requirements below are documented for this workflow. Unspecified limits are not inferred. [1] [3] [4]

Hardware
Minimum whole-workflow RAM, depth VRAM and total storage are not documented. Depth weights download about 1.3 GB, CLIPSeg/SAM about 1 GB together. Optional Qwen captions separately need about 6 GB free graphics memory and a 4.9 GB file; that is not a universal minimum for depth or the basic cube.
Software
Python 3.11+, ffmpeg/ffprobe and Chrome/Edge with WebGL2. Neural requirements are unpinned torch, NumPy/Pillow/OpenCV/SciPy/accelerate/safetensors plus transformers>=4.56,<5; Qwen captions separately require transformers 4.57. Independent dependency compatibility is unverified.
Platforms
Windows 10/11 is declared and Windows 11 owner-tested. A browser window does not establish Mac/Linux support. NVIDIA/CUDA is explicitly required by the selected depth implementation; CPU-only procedural rendering is a different route.

How it uses AI

U2-Net/rembg segmentation isolates a person or object before deterministic tone mapping turns it into animated document bars, toner and paper. The AI component is the subject mask; the document effect is procedural. [1] [3] [4]

Introduction

U2-Net/rembg segmentation isolates a person or object before deterministic tone mapping turns it into animated document bars, toner and paper. The AI component is the subject mask; the document effect is procedural. [1] [3] [4]

What it is good for

Creating stylized portrait loops, mock document graphics and paper-like motion plates from owned photos or clips. [1] [3] [4]

Demo & examples

The owner links a tutorial and several portrait/video examples, plus an owner-run export/UI test report. They document an inspectable effect without independent quality validation. This is decorative document/redaction styling, not verified removal of private information from a source document. [1] [3] [4]

Install

Use Python 3.11+, Windows 10/11, Chrome or Edge and ffmpeg/ffprobe on PATH. Install the current requirements in the intended Python environment, then launch the local app. CPU inference is supported; optional onnxruntime-gpu replaces onnxruntime and needs the documented cuDNN 9 path. The first person/object mask requests download separate U2-Net files. No app, package or model was installed or run for this review. [1] [3] [4]

First project

Start with a short, representative project and inspect the result before a larger batch. [1] [3] [4]

  1. Open or drop an owned photo or short video; hold Original to compare framing and tone.
  2. Choose Person or Object to request the corresponding U2-Net cut-out. Inspect the mask: current masks.py falls back to the whole picture on inference failure, so a completed render does not prove that subject segmentation succeeded.
  3. Choose a preset, adjust document type, redaction amount, toner and sheet-change interval; use matching framing when comparing different inputs.
  4. Preview the motion and step individual sheets. Export PNG for a still or H.264 MP4 for the loop/clip; the documented video route preserves input sound.
  5. Inspect the actual output at delivery size, including bars, fine edges, sound and text. An artistic effect and an owner test report do not certify confidentiality, segmentation accuracy or a target editor workflow.

How it uses AI

A local JSON/Rust/MCP editing engine lets an AI agent arrange recorded footage, revise a saved timeline, add captions/graphics, preview and export. It edits existing media rather than generating new video from a prompt. [1] [3] [4] [5]

Introduction

A local JSON/Rust/MCP editing engine lets an AI agent arrange recorded footage, revise a saved timeline, add captions/graphics, preview and export. It edits existing media rather than generating new video from a prompt. [1] [3] [4] [5]

What it is good for

Building inspectable interview, screen-tutorial and presentation edits with retained media and revision history. [1] [3] [4] [5]

Demo & examples

Owner evidence describes frame-exact cuts, retry/undo checks and repeat exports on a particular FFmpeg build. The linked competitor comparison includes source/document reviews for some alternatives, not equal hands-on trials for every tool. Independent full creative-workflow use remains unverified. [1] [3] [4] [5]

Install

For the scoped Windows/Linux source route, obtain the official source, use the owner-documented Rust edition2024-compatible toolchain plus C compiler/linker for SQLite, and provide FFmpeg/ffprobe on PATH or their explicit CUTBOLT variables. Install the locked project and inspect doctor output. Its setup-ready flag is not a render, GPU, speech or model test. The installation document still says binaries are in preparation while README describes early releases; this guide deliberately scopes source setup rather than treating the two as equivalent. [1] [3] [4] [5]

  1. Obtain the official source or release linked below.
  2. cargo install --path . --locked
cutbolt --version
cutbolt doctor
cutbolt --workspace /absolute/path/to/workspace mcp

First project

Start with a short, representative project and inspect the result before a larger batch. [1] [3] [4] [5]

  1. Create a separate media/output workspace and connect the stdio server from the same environment that finds FFmpeg. Keep the selected source commit and media-tool build with the project.
  2. Ask the agent to inspect an owned clip, trim its first ten seconds, save the edit and show a preview. This is the owner’s first-use task, not a completed test by AI Media Scout.
  3. Use the saved-session commands for shared revisions, retry receipts and undo; a pure timeline.apply result can fork when two callers edit the same supplied snapshot and is not a shared session lock.
  4. Inspect the preview and exported sound/timing. Optional speech recognition needs its own environment/models; a connected MCP endpoint or doctor-ready result does not establish transcription readiness.
  5. Review a small final export before a long job. The documented MCP export/background path needs Windows or Linux; an available macOS ARM64 executable alone does not clear that whole workflow.

Hardware & software

Requirements below are documented for this workflow. Unspecified limits are not inferred. [1] [3] [4] [5]

Hardware
Minimum system RAM, VRAM and total disk space are not documented in the reviewed instructions.
Software
Rust edition2024-compatible source toolchain, C compiler/linker and external FFmpeg/ffprobe. Python, speech checkpoints, CUDA/WSL and interchange tools are optional workflow/development dependencies, not universal core requirements.
Platforms
Windows x64 and Linux x64 are current verification platforms. macOS ARM64 archives exist, but background/MCP export and Windows-specific engine features have narrower support. Client/plugin coverage is also version- and platform-scoped.

License, model weights & costs

The full software license was reviewed. Model weights, assets and services remain separate. [2] [1] [3] [4] [5]

  • Weights: Full MIT engine grant reviewed. FFmpeg/codecs, fonts, optional Whisper/CTC weights, proprietary GPU drivers and the selected agent host retain separate terms. The dependency ledger’s RTX4090/WSL speech setup is one tested owner profile, not a required or independently reproduced minimum.
  • Commercial: Software use follows MIT. Full MIT engine grant reviewed. FFmpeg/codecs, fonts, optional Whisper/CTC weights, proprietary GPU drivers and the selected agent host retain separate terms. The dependency ledger’s RTX4090/WSL speech setup is one tested owner profile, not a required or independently reproduced minimum.
  • Cost: No hosted-service account is required by the core local engine. Hardware and optional model/agent/provider costs are separate.

Why it merits attention

Included for the documented creative workflow. A complete guide and popularity do not establish output reliability. [1] [3] [4] [5]

Limitations

The core default is 8-bit SDR; optional working-space/effect/source/interchange paths have separate contracts. Setup-ready, source preservation, deterministic receipts and owner acceptance tests do not establish output quality, current cross-client usability or independent support. Full APIs and the entire dependency ledger were not audited line by line; the guide covers the cited core setup/session route. [1] [3] [4] [5]

Scope: Arranging fixed-sheet laser/CNC/textile parts while preserving quantities, spacing, rotations and source geometry for CAM review.

  • Reproducible setup · documented: For the scoped basic SVG/DXF MCP route, obtain the official stable v0.1.0 native archive for Windows x64, Linux x64 or macOS Apple Silicon, verify checksums and place the executable on the intended path. Building current source instead selects Rust 1.98.1 with a Cargo minimum1.98 and may fetch large benchmark/upstream data. Independent reproduction has not been established in this review. [1] [3] [4]
  • Maintenance and support · documented: Current release/commit and bounded issue/PR evidence were inspected for workflow support.   • Independent use · unknown: No successful independent use of the complete selected workflow was established in the bounded support/primary-document review. Owner automation, bots, adjacent-workflow reports and popularity are not substituted. [5]
  • Models, services and costs · documented: Complete MIT project grant reviewed. jagua-rs remains MPL2; adapted Sparrow code is MIT. LightBurn/CAM hosts, stock data, fonts/artwork and the chosen AI client retain separate terms. The geometric MCP server itself documents no network/account/key requirement. How it uses AI

A local MCP interface lets an AI agent import part outlines, choose stock/constraints, call a deterministic nesting optimizer, validate and export cutting layouts. The optimizer is geometric rather than a neural model. [1] [3] [4]

Introduction

A local MCP interface lets an AI agent import part outlines, choose stock/constraints, call a deterministic nesting optimizer, validate and export cutting layouts. The optimizer is geometric rather than a neural model. [1] [3] [4]

What it is good for

Arranging fixed-sheet laser/CNC/textile parts while preserving quantities, spacing, rotations and source geometry for CAM review. [1] [3] [4]

Demo & examples

The owner’s frozen v1 benchmark reports 8.5% median material improvement over selected alternatives at 60 seconds, yet misses its own90%-wins/ties bar; Sparrow still wins some cases. An independent geometry validator is a separate algorithm, not an independent creative user or proof of physical cutting. The newer preview has no new like-for-like competitor claim. [1] [3] [4]

Install

For the scoped basic SVG/DXF MCP route, obtain the official stable v0.1.0 native archive for Windows x64, Linux x64 or macOS Apple Silicon, verify checksums and place the executable on the intended path. Register the absolute nestbolt-mcp path and set its workspace to the cutting-files folder. A browser UI is optional; it runs a newer preview and is not the same version. Building current source instead selects Rust 1.98.1 with a Cargo minimum1.98 and may fetch large benchmark/upstream data. [1] [3] [4]

  1. Obtain the official source or release linked below.
  2. For the scoped basic SVG/DXF MCP route, obtain the official stable v0.1.0 native archive for Windows x64, Linux x64 or macOS Apple Silicon, verify checksums and place the executable on the intended path. Register the absolute nestbolt-mcp path and set its workspace to the cutting-files folder. A browser UI is optional; it runs a newer preview and is not the same version. claude mcp add --transport stdio --scope local nestbolt -- /absolute/path/to/nestbolt-mcp

First project

Start with a short, representative project and inspect the result before a larger batch. [1] [3] [4]

  1. Start with the owner’s simple SVG parts in a separate workspace. Call import_parts and inspect units, part ids, holes, repairs and stock outlines before nesting.
  2. Supply quantities, sheet sizes/margins and spacing in millimetres, plus permitted rotations/grain direction and mirroring. estimate_sheets is only an area lower bound, never a proven feasible layout.
  3. Call nest with a bounded budget and seed, then validate_layout and export_layout. The five-tool interface is the same core as the CLI; a reported real-time limit can end deterministic-repeatability guarantees.
  4. Inspect each SVG/DXF in the actual CAM program and verify scale, contours and tool setup. Material geometry validity is not a machine-ready cut plan.
  5. Treat editable LightBurn import, lead allowances, remnant handling and common-line cutting as separately versioned preview/development workflows. Their limitations and current third-party DXF import reports must not be transferred into a blanket claim that the stable basic route is tested on every shop file.

Hardware & software

Requirements below are documented for this workflow. Unspecified limits are not inferred. [1] [3] [4]

Hardware
Minimum system RAM, VRAM and total disk space are not documented in the reviewed instructions.
Software
Prebuilt native tools need an MCP client/agent, with sufficient call timeout for up to 300 seconds. Optional source uses Rust 1.98.1 (Cargo declares 1.98 minimum); local WebAssembly UI development adds Node 24 and wasm-bindgen-cli0.2.129. No universal RAM/VRAM/storage minimum is documented.
Platforms
Native archives are published for Windows x64, Linux x64 and macOS Apple Silicon. Owner client checks have narrower platform/version scope; accepting one DeepSeek configuration was not an actual server start without that host’s key.

  • Commercial: Software use follows MIT. Complete MIT project grant reviewed. jagua-rs remains MPL2; adapted Sparrow code is MIT.
